### The Accident Changed the Air Data System, Eventually

**The aircraft that was destroyed by a probe returned with no probe.** The second aircraft flew again from
2001 to 2003 in the Vectoring Extremely Short Takeoff and Landing Control Operation Research programme,
which used thrust vectoring to fly approaches at high angle of attack, and that aircraft carried a
**flush air data system** with no protruding probe to ice.

That is a satisfying arc and it should not be overstated. Flush air data systems were under development for
other reasons, principally for hypersonic vehicles where a probe cannot survive, and the X-31's second life
was a separate programme with its own objectives.
**But the pairing is real, and the flush system removes the exact failure mode that destroyed the first aircraft**,
because there is no single pneumatic path whose blockage can be shared by every computer downstream.

The Mishap Board's own recommendations were institutional rather than technical for the most part. It
recommended that the system safety analyses be redone with a focus on single-point failures, that a
mitigation for the loss of raw pitot-static data be found and verified in simulation, that temporary
operating procedures be reviewed and controlled, that all cockpit switches and displays be audited for
correct function and labelling, that programme personnel be trained in the basics of system safety, and that
the parachute be replaced with one of slower descent rate.

**The last of those is not a control-system finding and it belongs in the record.** The pilot descended
under a seventeen-foot canopy at approximately 28.3 feet per second, a rate typical for naval aircraft that
operate mostly over water, and was injured on the hard playa surface.

### What Some Test Team Members Thought the Report Missed

**Not everyone accepted the Mishap Report's emphasis**, and the disagreement is instructive. Team members
felt that large airspeed errors and suspected icing should have mandated stopping the test until the
discrepancy was understood, and that the report placed too little weight on the test team's own methodology.
One is quoted saying that the test pilot and flight control engineers ought to understand the importance of
the right gains, and another that seventy-knot errors and icing conditions are not acceptable for good
flight tests.

**The lesson the programme's own pilots drew was procedural rather than technical.** Rogers Smith put it to
the Society of Experimental Test Pilots in 1996 as an instruction to speak up, stop the tests and think when
something does not make sense, and Ken Szalai, the Dryden director at the time, put it as calling a halt for
anything that does not sound, feel or smell right.

**Both formulations are about a failure of attention rather than a failure of engineering**, and the
arithmetic in this article supports that reading. The information needed to detect the failure was
displayed, spoken aloud, and independently available on a second instrument.

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

**Treating this aircraft through its keystone misleads in five places, and the first is the sharpest.**

### The Exchange Ratio Is Not a Property of the Aircraft

**The whole apparatus of this article treats the exchange ratio as a measurement, and it is better understood as a joint measurement of an aircraft, a pilot, a tactic, an adversary and a set of rules.**
The programme's own data proves it. The same aircraft in the same simulator with the same starting condition
returned 1.2 in one campaign and 0.47 in the next, and the programme attributed the difference to which
initial move the pilots chose.

**A number that moves by a factor of two and a half when the pilots change their minds is not a property of the airframe.**

### The Adversary Chooses the Engagement

**The F-15C and F-16C result is the boundary of the entire keystone.** An advantage that exists inside a
slow, close fight is realisable only if the opponent enters one. Against aircraft with enough excess thrust
to sit above the fight and re-enter at their chosen moment, the X-31's advantage was largely unavailable.

**This is an old lesson and the NASA history says so**, drawing the parallel with the [Fokker Dr I][ref_dr1]
of 1917, an aircraft capable of post-stall pointing that pilots of faster conventional fighters learned to
defeat by extending and attacking in slashing passes, denying it the ability to choose the time and place of
the fight.

### The Demonstrator Was Underpowered

**The X-31 fought the F/A-18 from a position of disadvantage in conventional performance**, and the NASA
history says so directly, attributing an exchange ratio of 0.37 without post-stall technology to the basic
mediocrity of an underpowered design against a more powerful opponent with a better dogfighting wing.

**That cuts both ways and the second direction is the more interesting.** An aircraft that loses almost
three fights in four conventionally and wins nearly two in one with post-stall technology has demonstrated a
large effect. But it has demonstrated it on an airframe whose conventional performance nobody would accept,
and the programme's own conclusion is that post-stall technology must be an integrated element of a design
rather than a bolt-on.

### The Angle of Attack Limit Is Not the Whole Capability

The article has treated the usable angle of attack as the figure of merit, because it is what sets the
aim-off angle. **The programme's own conclusions put velocity-vector roll rate alongside it**, and describe
the main advantages as apparent directional nose-pointing rate, which is actually yaw rate or
velocity-vector roll capability at high angle of attack, together with very high nose authority in pitch.

**Pointing where you cannot get to quickly is worth less than the static aim-off angle suggests**, and a
full treatment would need the rate as well as the angle. The rate is not published in a form this article
could use.

### The Simulation and Flight Numbers Are Not the Same Experiment

**The exchange ratios analysed at length in this article are simulation results**, from Pinball I and
Pinball II. The flight programme reported 407 engagements of which 325 were scored, and its conclusions are
stated qualitatively. **The temptation to read the simulator's 1.83 as a flight result should be resisted**,
and the widely quoted figures of thirty to one and of nearly ten to one over 103 dogfights come from yet
other accountings whose provenance this article has not been able to trace to a primary source.

**That inconsistency is itself the finding.** A programme whose keystone was a measured rate produced at
least four different headline rates that circulate independently, and the arithmetic above shows why that is
easy. The pooled value depends on the mix of starting conditions, and nobody quoting a single number says
which mix produced it.

**The condition under which pooling reverses a conclusion can be written down.** A set of per-condition
ratios supports a claim of advantage on average while the pooled figure denies it whenever

$$\frac{1}{N} \sum_i E_i > 1 \qquad \text{and} \qquad \sum_i w_i E_i < 1$$

which is possible for any set that contains at least one ratio below parity, because the weights are free.
For the published set the two sides are 6.43 and 1.83, so both inequalities are satisfied with room to spare
in the first and by a margin of 0.83 in the second.
**The margin is what the knife-edge calculation above measures**, and it is smaller than the uncertainty in
the weights. The general form of that hazard is known in statistics as
[the reversal that can occur when groups are pooled][ref_simpson].

## The Contemporary Literature

**The X-31 flew before three of the fields that now own its questions existed.** This section surveys the
modern literature on each, written from current knowledge rather than from the editorial date of the
article.

### Air combat decision making became a machine learning problem

**The question the X-31 answered with six test pilots and a scoring radar is now answered with learned policies.**
Reinforcement learning agents flying within-visual-range engagements are a substantial and growing
literature, and they inherit the X-31's problem exactly. An agent trained against one adversary policy and
one distribution of starting conditions learns an advantage conditional on both, and the aggregate win rate
it reports is the same weighted mean this article has spent its keystone section unpacking.

**The modern work has one advantage the X-31 did not.** It can fly ten thousand engagements rather than four
hundred, which moves the high-speed line-abreast case from unresolvable to routine.

### Pursuit and evasion had a formal theory the programme did not use

**The mathematical treatment of one-versus-one air combat as a differential game predates the X-31 by decades**,
with [Isaacs on differential games][book_isaacs] as its founding text, and the programme's reports do not
appear to draw on it. That literature poses the engagement as a two-sided optimisation with a value
function, and the value at a given starting state is precisely the quantity the exchange ratio estimates by
sampling.

**A formal treatment would have said in advance that the value depends on the starting state**, which is the
article's central finding arrived at by arithmetic on published outcomes. The two literatures were solving
the same problem in different buildings.

### Unmanned combat aircraft made the tailless problem central

**The configuration question the quasi-tailless experiment opened is now the dominant one.** A combat
aircraft designed for low observability wants no vertical surfaces, and an aircraft with no vertical
surfaces has no directional stiffness and no rudder. The modern literature on yaw effectors, split ailerons,
differential drag devices and vectoring for tailless configurations is a direct continuation.

### Control allocation became a discipline

**An aircraft with more effectors than axes has to decide how to use them**, and the X-31 had exactly that
problem with elevons, canards, rudder and three vectoring paddles. It solved it with hand-tuned mixing in
thirty-two software releases. The modern answer is control allocation as an optimisation solved at frame
rate, with constraints for position and rate limits of the kind this article computed for the vectoring
vanes.

**Incremental nonlinear dynamic inversion is the other major change.** The X-31's control laws were reactive
and scheduled on measured flight condition, which is precisely the architecture whose failure destroyed the
first aircraft. Incremental methods reduce the dependence on a model of the aircraft and therefore on the
accuracy of the air data that parameterises it.

### High angle of attack aerodynamics is computed now, with reservations

**The flow the X-31 could not predict is now computed**, and analyses of the flow around the X-31 itself at
high angle of attack have been published as validation cases decades after the aircraft stopped flying.
**That is a fitting use of the data.** An aircraft that flew because the tunnel could not settle a question
has become the benchmark against which the calculation that replaced the tunnel is judged.

The reservations are real. Forebody vortex asymmetry is sensitive to a nose radius of half an inch, to
boundary-layer transition state and to Reynolds number, and those are the three things computation finds
hardest.

### Air data without a probe

**Flush air data sensing became a field**, driven by hypersonic vehicles where a probe cannot survive and by
low-observable aircraft where a probe is a radar reflector. The methods estimate free-stream conditions from
a distributed array of surface pressure ports through a model, frequently a learned one.

**The relevance to this article is direct.** A distributed array has no single pneumatic path whose blockage
is shared by every downstream computer, which is the exact architectural property whose absence killed the
first X-31.

### Analytical redundancy and sensor fault detection

**The cross-check this article performed by hand is now a standard technique with a literature.** Analytical
redundancy uses the known dynamics of the aircraft to predict what one sensor should read from what the
others read, and flags a disagreement. Applied to the accident flight it would have compared angle of attack
against airspeed through the lift curve and raised a fault when the two disagreed by eleven degrees.

**Nothing in that technique required computing power the X-31 did not have.** It required someone to have
written down the relation.

### Envelope protection and loss of control

**Loss of control in flight remains the largest single category of fatal accident in several sectors**, and
the modern response is envelope estimation and protection computed in real time. The X-31 sits at an
interesting point in that history, since carefree handling is envelope protection by another name and the
aircraft demonstrated both its value and its cost.
**A pilot who cannot depart also cannot feel the approach to departure.**

### Agility as a measured quantity

**The vocabulary of agility metrics was being invented while the X-31 flew**, and the modern literature has
largely settled on the distinctions the period was reaching for, separating pointing agility from axial and
torsional agility. The X-31's contribution to that vocabulary was to make the pointing component large
enough to measure cleanly.

**This is the thinnest contemporary heading in the article and it is reported rather than padded.** A
harvest aimed directly at it returned six records published from 2015 onward against a period holding of
fifteen. The subject did not disappear. It dissolved into trajectory optimisation and into autonomous
manoeuvre selection, where the question is no longer what metric describes agility but what an agent does
with it.

### Post-stall migrated to small aircraft

**The manoeuvre the X-31 needed sixteen thousand pounds of thrust to perform is now flown by model aircraft.**
Perching manoeuvres, post-stall attitude control and aggressive trajectory following on small unmanned
aircraft are an active field, and the physics is favourable at small scale because the ratio of available
thrust to weight is easy to make large and the energy recovery time scales with the speed.

### Uncertainty quantification

**A result with an interval attached is a modern expectation and it was not a 1993 one.** The X-31 published
point estimates of exchange ratios with no uncertainty statement, and the arithmetic in this article's
keystone section is an attempt to supply retrospectively what the discipline now supplies as a matter of
course.

### Weapons, displays and the off-boresight problem

**The programme's own recommendation was that post-stall technology needs a missile that can be launched off boresight at high angle of attack**,
and that combination arrived shortly afterwards and changed close combat more than the airframe technology
did. An aircraft that can point its seeker without pointing itself does not need to point itself.

**That is the strongest single argument against the X-31's own premise**, and the programme identified it.

### The vehicle's own literature

**This cluster is the one place where the contemporary survey has nothing to survey.** No record published
from 2015 onward in the harvested pool is about the X-31 itself, which is what one should expect of an
aircraft that stopped flying in 2003. The programme's own literature is therefore listed here in full rather
than split by era, and the modern work that touches the aircraft appears under computational aerodynamics
above, where it uses the X-31 as a validation case rather than studying the aeroplane.

## The Source Base

**The reference set behind this article holds 450 records published through 1999 and 524 published from 2015 onward**,
drawn from a master set of anchored records assembled from the NASA Technical Reports Server, the Defense
Technical Information Center through its registered identifiers, and the Crossref registry across journal
and conference literature.

**The count and the fraction are both reported, because they move in opposite directions.** Adding a
contemporary survey holds the period count and lowers its fraction, and adding period sources holds the
contemporary count and lowers its fraction. Neither movement is a fact about coverage. Both are facts about
the denominator.

### What the Harvest Missed the First Time

**The first two harvests filtered the Crossref query to journal articles, and this aircraft did not publish in journals.**
The X-31 was reported almost entirely at meetings of the American Institute of Aeronautics and Astronautics
and the Society of Experimental Test Pilots, and at the 1994 High Alpha Conference. The vehicle cluster
stood at eighteen records while the two most important papers about the programme sat in the registry
unqueried.

**Dropping the type filter and asking again moved the vehicle cluster from eighteen to twenty-seven anchored records and added six hundred and thirty-eight conference records overall**,
including the tactical utility paper the keystone rests on and the close-in-combat results assessment
published a decade after the programme ended.

### What the Cluster Audit Found Before Writing

**Auditing the pool against the article's topic list before writing rather than after is a rule this series earned expensively.**
Four clusters came back thin and each carried a claim the article rests on. Unstable dynamics stood at four
records against a section that rests on a time to double. Handling qualities stood at eleven against the
carefree-handling argument. Agility metrics stood at twelve and control power at sixteen, against the whole
of the pointing and authority analysis. Two further harvests aimed at those four raised them to 38, 216, 21
and 34.

**A second audit, run against the assembled draft rather than against the pool, found a defect of a kind this series had not met before.**
Thirteen cluster-and-era pairs held fewer records than the draft cited, and
**twelve of the thirteen were the modern half**. The cause was not a thin heading and not a thin subject. It
was a thin **era**, because the first harvests asked the modern pool only for the subjects that are
obviously modern, being machine learning, autonomy and uncertainty, and asked the period pool for everything
else. The contemporary literature on subjects that existed in 1993 and still exist was never requested at
all. A harvest written against that diagnosis moved the modern pool from 1,316 records to 4,947.

**The one cluster that stayed empty is the aircraft's own.** No record published from 2015 onward is about
the X-31 itself, and that is a fact about an aircraft which stopped flying in 2003 rather than a defect in a
query, so it is reported and not chased.

### A Defect in the Selection That Would Have Narrowed Everything

**The cluster patterns carried a systematic word-boundary defect and it was silently discarding records.** A
pattern of the form "agility followed by metric or measure or parameter" closed with a word boundary fails
on the phrase "agility metrics", because the boundary after "metric" requires a non-word character and the
letter "s" is not one. The same defect appeared in twenty alternation groups across the file, affecting
stems such as "generat", "estimat" and "calibrat" and singular nouns such as "strake", "derivative" and
"coefficient".

**A second and independent variant was the hyphen.** Titles are written "High Angle-of-Attack", "Flush
Air-Data" and "vortex-burst" while the patterns were written with literal spaces, so a paper titled "High
Angle-of-Attack Aerodynamics" fell through to the no-cluster pile.

**Both were found by reading a random sample of the discarded records rather than by inspecting the patterns**,
which is the only method that has ever worked for this class of defect.

### On the Primary Record

**The primary record for this aircraft is unusually good in one respect and unusually poor in another.** The
flight reports, the quasi-tailless results, the yawing-moment asymmetry analysis and the tactical utility
paper are all public, detailed and quantitative. The NASA history published in 2014 carries a complete
per-flight log for both aircraft and the simulation exchange ratios by starting condition.

**What is not public is the underlying count of engagements and outcomes per condition**, which is the one
thing needed to attach an interval to any of the published ratios without assumption. This article works
around that by asking what various sample sizes would support rather than by guessing which one applied.

## Epistemic State

**Historical fact, from the primary and official record.**

- Two aircraft were built by Rockwell International and Messerschmitt-Bolkow-Blohm and flew from
  October 1990 to June 1995, logging 580 flights with fourteen pilots.
- The programme was managed by the Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency and flown by an
  International Test Organization at NASA Dryden, and was the first experimental aircraft
  programme administered by a United States agency as an international effort.
- Controlled flight at seventy degrees of angle of attack was achieved on 6 November 1992, and a
  minimum-radius one-hundred-and-eighty-degree post-stall turn on 29 April 1993.
- The first aircraft was lost on 19 January 1995 following ice accretion on an unheated Kiel
  probe, with the pilot ejecting and being injured on landing.
- The published simulation exchange ratios against the F/A-18 with post-stall technology are 0.36,
  8.5, 16 and 0.86 across four starting conditions, with an overall figure of 1.83.
- The tactical utility flight test flew 87 sorties of which 80 were scored, producing 407
  engagements of which 325 were scored.
- The quasi-tailless demonstration reached a time to double amplitude of 290 milliseconds at
  complete simulated tail removal at a supersonic condition.
- The second aircraft flew again from 2001 to 2003 in a separate programme with a flush air data
  system.

**Engineering analysis performed for this article and verified independently.**

- The pooled exchange ratio is the mean of the per-condition ratios weighted by the losses of the
  denominator side. This is an identity and it is checked as a randomised property over twenty
  thousand random count vectors.
- Between 81.9 and 93.6 percent of the aircraft's simulated losses must have occurred in the two
  starting conditions where it was behind. The bracket is derived analytically from two-point
  mixtures and confirmed by Monte Carlo over twenty-seven thousand feasible weight vectors, which
  ran from 0.8198 to 0.9356.
- At twelve scored outcomes per condition the defensive and high-speed line-abreast results do not
  separate from parity. The high-speed line-abreast ratio of 0.86 would need 691 scored outcomes
  to separate, against a few dozen flown.
- The loop gain on the accident flight stood between 2.890 and 12.543 times its design value,
  against a conventional gain margin factor of 1.995.
- The displayed airspeed and the vane angle of attack disagreed by 11.2 degrees at the moment the
  pilot remarked on them, corresponding to a dynamic-pressure error of 2.266, which already
  exceeded the design gain margin.
- Vectored control authority at thirty thousand feet is 0.6462 of its value at twenty thousand at
  the same calibrated airspeed, a loss of 35.4 percent.
- The post-stall regime offers no turn-radius advantage. Every radius computed at seventy degrees
  of angle of attack exceeds the 1,171 feet available at corner speed.
- The energy given up in a post-stall reversal is 4,600 feet of specific energy and takes 44.7
  seconds to recover, integrated.

**Inference, where the record supports a conclusion without stating it.**

- The moment arm from the centre of gravity to the vane station is inferred at 16.22 feet by
  requiring the available vectoring authority to equal the measured forebody asymmetry at the
  condition where departures occurred. The implied centre of gravity at 60.1 percent of fuselage
  length is geometrically reasonable for a canard delta, which is why the inference is offered as
  a check rather than as a measurement.
- The lift-curve slope of 2.769 per radian is inferred from a test pilot's recollection of a trim
  point rather than from published data, and is supported by falling between the unswept and
  swept low-aspect-ratio estimates.
- The altitude dependence of vectored authority is offered as the quantitative explanation of an
  effect the flight-test report describes qualitatively. The report does not make the connection.

**Claims the record does not settle.**

- **The number of engagements behind each published exchange ratio is not public**, so every
  interval in this article is conditional on an assumed sample size.
- **Two widely circulated figures could not be traced to a primary source.** They are
  approximately thirty to one, and nearly ten to one over 103 dogfights. Neither is among the
  simulation figures analysed here and the provenance of both is unresolved.
- **The effect of an airframe with adequate conventional performance is unknown.** The
  programme's own conclusion that post-stall technology must be integrated rather than added
  implies the exchange ratio would have differed.
- The yaw inertia is not published, so the directional stiffness of the simulated tailless
  configuration is determined only as a ratio.
- The velocity-vector roll rate achieved at seventy degrees of angle of attack is described
  qualitatively in the sources consulted and no usable figure was found, so the pointing analysis
  in this article treats the angle and not the rate.
- **The basis of the 2004 close-in-combat results assessment was not established.** Whether it
  reports flight or simulation figures, and under what accounting, is unresolved, because the
  paper was identified through the registry rather than read in full.

## Out of Scope

- The detailed structural design of the wing and the German certification arrangements behind it.
- The Vectoring Extremely Short Takeoff and Landing programme of 2001 to 2003 in its own right,
  which is a distinct programme with distinct objectives and is treated here only where it bears
  on air data.
- The F/A-18 High Alpha Research Vehicle and the F-16 multi-axis thrust vectoring programme, both
  contemporaneous and both deserving separate treatment.
- Radar cross-section and low-observable design, which is the reason the tailless result matters
  and is not itself an X-31 subject.
- The design of the missiles the programme concluded were necessary.
- The organisational history of the International Test Organization beyond what bears on the
  flight-test method.

## Conclusion

**The X-31 was built to find out whether an aeroplane that can point its nose forty degrees further round than its opponent wins, and it found out that the answer depends on where the fight starts.**

That is a less satisfying result than the reputation, and it is a better one. The programme produced four
exchange ratios against a conventional adversary and the aircraft lost two of them. It produced an overall
figure of 1.83 which is a loss-weighted average dominated by the two it lost, and which sits three and a
half times below the average of the four.
**Between eighty-two and ninety-four percent of its simulated losses occurred in the conditions where post-stall manoeuvring did not help.**

It produced, in flight against front-line fighters, a clear advantage over the Navy types that would fight
it close and a clear disadvantage against the Air Force types that would not.

**An exchange ratio is not a property of an aeroplane. It is a property of an engagement**, and the
adversary has a say in which engagement occurs. The X-31 measured that with more care than anyone had
before, and the measurement is usually quoted as a single number by people who did not read the table it
came from.

**The programme also produced a result nobody was looking for that has outlasted the one it was built for.**
An aircraft with no vertical tail at all can be held at supersonic speed by vectoring, diverging at 290
milliseconds to double. That is now the central configuration problem of combat aircraft design and the X-31
answered it in 1994 as a side experiment made cheap by having already integrated vectoring into the control
laws.

**And it produced a lesson about instrumentation that cost an aeroplane.** A probe fitted to solve a real
problem at high angle of attack was not heated, its cockpit switch was not placarded, the hazard analysis
was not repeated, and the flight control system had no way to notice that both of its air data computers
were being fed the same wrong number. The information needed to catch it was displayed on the head-up
display, spoken aloud over the intercom, available on a standby instrument near the pilot's knee, and
computable in one line from the lift equation.
**None of those was a redundancy the design had asked for, and any one of them would have been enough.**
